A. Introduction

We assume that Sanskrit grammar is known to the reader. It should not matter much whether he is coming from a traditional (Pāṇinian) or Western grammatical background. In both systems, the accent is treated extensively — though it’s not taught in most Sanskrit courses, as it’s rarely used in Classical Sanskrit.

Though my own limited familiarity with the Aṣṭādhyāyī means that I’ll include less of the traditional terminology, examples should make things amply clear.

Once you’re done with the Notation (I) section below, do one of the following to learn the pronunciation of svara-s. I don’t wish to stress on this too much, and śākhā-s differ enough in pronunciation to make the second (easy) option correct.

  1. Watch this video. How to Chant the Veda Mantras Correctly : The Story of Indra and Vritrasura - 3 Swaras Explained

  2. Easy: Pronounce udā́tta at high pitch, and everything else at low pitch. That’s it.

Feel free to make a copy of this document, or to create a PDF of it. Direct any queries to me on Twitter

B. Notation (I) {#b.-notation-(i)}

We should get this out of the way as soon as possible, so that we can get to the juicy stuff (grammar).

First, there are 3 levels of svára (or accent) that vowels have – udā́tta, ánudātta, and svaritá. Complexity arises from the fact that the first of these — udā́tta — is grammatically primary but not explicitly indicated in the most common system of writing Vedic svára-s in Devanāgarī [which we will follow]. But it is prominently notated in English transcription by an ‘acute accent mark’ — as the á in gatá- “gone”.

So, what does it mean to say that the udā́tta is grammatically primary? It’s the one determined first by certain grammatical rules [which we’ll learn in the following sections], or just known by tradition [i.e. innately]. For example, the word gata- “gone” is observed to be a simple past participle — this alone is enough to determine that the suffix (-ta-) receives the udā́tta, hence gatá-. Contrariwise, the accentuation of kitavá- “gambler” just is.

But this rule says nothing about the other vowels in a word or sentence! The fundamental rule for those can be summarised as follows: All vowels that are not udā́tta are ánudātta, but a sequence UA → US (where U, S, A stand for the respective svára-s). However, UAU remains unchanged.

For example, in the word suṣéṇa-, the transcription clearly indicates that the e is udā́tta. Then, as a first step, the u and a are ánudātta. But the a then becomes svaritá (because UA → US). In Devanāgarī, these two are indicated as follows: सु॒षेण॑-.

Observe the following examples. Hereon we will write in both scripts side by side.

  • भा॒गः bhāgáḥ “part”
  • सु॒प॒र्ण- suparṇá- “having beautiful leaves [or wings]”
  • च॒रा॒च॒र- carācará- “moving
  • कामः॑ kā́maḥ “desire”
  • दे॒वता॑ devátā “deity”
  • मे॒हना॑ mehánā “abundantly”

The next major Devanāgarī notational convention is: Except in a series of ánudātta-s at the start of a sentence (say, AAAU), only that ánudātta is written which immediately precedes an udā́tta. For example, in áśvaparṇa- “flying using horses”, the underlying form is UAAA, which became USAA (by the rule UA → US); now neither of the two ánudātta-s precede an udā́tta, so they’re not indicated in Devanāgarī at all. We write अश्व॑पर्ण-.

Observe the following illustrative examples:

  • अवि॑द्वान् ávidvān “not knowing”
  • द्यावा॑पृथि॒वी dyā́vāpṛthivī́ “heaven & earth” [with two udā́tta-s!]
  • वा॒मजा॑तः vāmájātaḥ “lovely by nature”
  • इ॒न्द्रा॒ग्नी indrāgnī́ “Indra & Agni”
  • सु॒र॒भिष्ट॑मः surabhíṣṭamaḥ “most fragrant”
  • शु॒शु॒लूक॑यातुः śuśulū́kayātuḥ “owl-demon”

Exercise : It’s good practice to start with some words in Roman transcription and attempt to produce their Devanāgarī forms. Try your hand at the following. Answers are at the bottom of this section.

ardháḥ “half” vívācāḥ “speaking variously” bṛhádreṇuḥ “stirring up thick dust”
sárgaḥ “stream” prāvitā́ “protector” reṇúḥ “dust”
dáridraḥ “poor” sáṁkāśaḥ “appearance” nimeṣáḥ “wink”
mahāgrāmáḥ “great multitude” āśréṣaḥ, a certain evil being huraścít “crooked one”

Of course, these rules actually apply to sentences [and, in the case of poetry, ardharcas i.e. hemistiches], once the udā́tta-s of the words are determined from grammatical rules. So observe the following bits from Ṛgveda I.1 and convince yourself of the rules at play.

  • होता॑रं रत्न॒धात॑मम् hótāraṃ ratnadhā́tamam
  • स दे॒वाँ एह व॑क्षति sá devā́m̐ éhá vakṣati
  • अ॒ग्निना॑ र॒यिम॑श्नव॒त्पोष॑मे॒व दि॒वेदि॑वे agnínā rayím aśnavat póṣam evá divé-dive

Reverse engineering from the written Devanāgarī text to determine the U, A, S can be annoying to beginners — the most useful rubrics are that (1) AX means X=U, and (2) XS means X=U. Here X stands for “unmarked”. Convince yourself of these on the basis of the Notation rules given at the start of this section.

So an udā́tta is generally “flanked” by an ánudātta on its left and a svaritá on its right. But said ánudātta or svaritá can be altered under the influence of the other vowels in the utterance, in accordance with the Notation rules.
[Another fact that helps is that an unmarked vowel must be either an udā́tta or an ánudātta. If one can confirm or exclude either possibility, the job is done.]

A word generally has 1 (one) udā́tta, but often 0 and sometimes 2.

More examples to practice:

  • दे॒वो दे॒वेभि॒रा ग॑मत् devó devébhir ā́ gamat
    “May the god come with (other) gods.”

  • उप॑ त्वाग्ने दि॒वेदि॑वे॒ दोषा॑वस्तर्धि॒या व॒यम् úpa tvāgne divé-dive dóṣāvastar dhiyā́ vayám
    “To you, dispeller of the night, O Agni, day by day with prayer …”

  • वर्ध॑मानं॒ स्वे दमे॑ várdhamānaṃ své dáme
    “increasing in one’s own abode”

  • नमो॒ भर॑न्त॒ एम॑सि námo bháranta émasi
    “Bringing [you] reverence, we come.”

  • स इद्दे॒वेषु॑ गच्छति sá íd devéṣu gacchati
    “He, verily, goes to the gods.”

Please feel free to shoot me a tweet (@dxrsam_0) if some doubt persists or if you feel I should explain something better.

C. High-value items

Here we’ll cover the most widely applicable grammatical rules of Vedic accent placement, without worrying too much about exceptions.

C.1. The giga-default accent of all words is final,

so अ॒र्धः ardháḥ “half”, व॒रः varáḥ “suitor”, ग॒त- gatá- “gone”, प्री॒तिः prītíḥ “pleasure”, and so on.

So recklessly follow this if you know nothing else. However, at even a slightly more advanced stage, one should learn a number of rules.

C.2. Given the accent of a base word,

all of its inflected forms retain that accent position. This pattern is said to be “columnar accent”. So कृ॒ष्णः kṛṣṇáḥ “black” (Nom. sg.), once known, automatically gives the case-forms कृ॒ष्णम् kṛṣṇám, कृ॒ष्णेन॑ kṛṣṇéna, कृ॒ष्णाय॑ kṛṣṇā́ya, and so on. So one can unambiguously write the stem (प्राति॑पदिकम्) as कृ॒ष्ण- kṛṣṇá-.
![][image1]
![][image2]
(Tables from Macdonell’s 1916 “A Vedic grammar for Students”,
here and elsewhere in this document)

This is generally applicable when changing the gender of adjectives as well. So कृ॒ष्णम् kṛṣṇám, कृ॒ष्णा kṛṣṇā́, and their case-forms retain this accent position, e.g. कृ॒ष्णया॑ kṛṣṇáyā, कृ॒ष्णायै॑ kṛṣṇā́yai, कृ॒ष्णाया॑म् kṛṣṇā́yām.

This general rule also applies to thematic verbs (i.e. those whose stem ends in a, viz. gaṇa-s 1, 4, 6, 10, as well as passive and causative verbs), so

  • gaṇa 1 : भर॑ति bhárati, भर॑न्ति bháranti, भर॑ते bhárate, भरे॑ bháre, etc.
  • gaṇa 4 : लि॒म्पति॑ limpáti, लि॒म्पन्ति॑ limpánti, लि॒म्पते॑ limpáte, लि॒म्पे limpé, etc.
  • passive : क्रि॒यते॑ kriyáte, क्रि॒यन्ते॑ kriyánte, etc.
  • causative : पा॒ठय॑ति pāṭháyati, पा॒ठय॑न्ति pāṭháyanti, etc.

![][image3]

In fact, the exact accent position simply depends on the gaṇa: root in 1 & 4, suffix in 6, 10, as well as passive (-yá-) and causative (-áya-) verbs. The future tense of all verbs (-ṣyá-) belongs here.

[Here “suffix” refers to the addition made to the root before the personal endings are added. In पा॒ठय॑ति pāṭháyati, the root is pāṭh- (in some ablaut grade), the suffix is -áya-, and the personal ending is -ti.]

Exercise : Inflect √गम् gam “go”, √चुर् cur “steal”, √तुद् tud “push” normally.
Then inflect any one of them in the passive and future as well.

This rule has important exceptions, treated now.

C.2.a. Athematic monosyllabic nouns generally shift the accent to the suffix in the weak cases (i.e. the non-sarvanāmasthāna cases). This means nouns of one akṣara whose stem ends in something other than a and ā. Such words are few, but one should not skip this basic class.

e.g. For नौः náuḥ “boat” we get the strong case-forms नाव॑म् nā́vam (Acc. sg.), नावः॑ nā́vaḥ‌ (Nom. pl.), but the weak case-forms ना॒वा nāvā́ (Ins. sg.), नौ॒भिः naubhíḥ (Ins. pl.), etc.

Similarly, for पाद्- pā́d- “foot”, we get
strong forms पाद॑म् pā́dam (Acc. sg.), पादौ॑ pā́dau (Nom.-Acc. du.), पादः॑ pā́daḥ (Nom. pl.)
weak forms: प॒दा padā́ (Ins. sg.), प॒द्भ्याम् padbhyā́m (Ins.-Dat.-Abl. du.), प॒दोः padóḥ (Gen.-Loc. du.), प॒दः pádaḥ (Acc. pl.), प॒द्भिः padbhíḥ (Ins. pl.), प॒त्सु patsú (Loc. pl.)
[Recall that पाद्- pā́d- shortens its vowel in the weak case-forms, to पद्- pád-, cf. the sū́tra ६.१.६३. This means the accent & ablaut shift in tandem.]

This behavior of the accent is known as “mobile accent”.

Exercise : Write the full declension table of वाच्- vā́c- “speech”. There is no ablaut.
Then do the same for आप्- ā́p- “water”. It does show ablaut (same as in पाद्- pā́d- “foot”).

But some athematic monosyllabic nouns continue to follow columnar accent. The two important ones are

  • गौः gáuḥ “cow”, e.g. Ins. sg. गवा॑ gávā, Gen. pl. गवा॑म् gávām
  • श्वा śvā́ “dog”, e.g. Ins. sg. शुना॑ śúnā, Ins. pl. श्वभिः॑ śvábhiḥ

However, the most important appearance of mobile accent appears with active participles of verbs — whenever the word is oxytone, e.g. लि॒म्पन्त्- limpánt- “smearing” [may be cited as लि॒म्पत्- limpát-] from लि॒म्पति॑ limpáti, the accent moves to a weak case ending whenever it starts with a vowel. So लि॒म्पन्त॑म् limpántam (Acc. sg.) but लि॒म्प॒ता limpatā́ (Ins. sg.), लि॒म्प॒ते limpaté (Dat. sg.), लि॒म्पद्भिः॑ limpádbhiḥ (Ins. pl.), etc.

Exercise : Write the full declension table of भु॒ञ्जन्त्- bhuñjánt- “enjoying”, the present active participle of √भुज् bhuj.

Aside : In the Ṛgveda, rule C.2.a is particularly evident in infinitives in -e, e.g. त्वि॒षे tviṣé “to be excited”, यु॒धे yudhé “to fight”, यु॒जे yujé “to join”. But when compounded, these stems are no longer monosyllabic, so आ॒दिशे॑ ādíśe, र॒क्षो॒युजे॑ rakṣoyúje, etc.

Aṣṭādhyāyī : See

  • ६.१.१७१ ऊडिदम्पदाद्यप्पुम्रैद्युभ्यः

  • ६.१.१७० अञ्चेश्छन्दस्यसर्वनामस्थानम्

C.2.b. Athematic verbs (in the present tense) systematically shift their accent position. This means verbs of gaṇas 2, 3, 5, 7, 8, 9.

The rule is that the stem is accented in the strong persons (these are 1sg., 2sg., and 3sg. in the active voice), while the ending is accented elsewhere. We look at examples:

  • For √अद् ad “eat” (gaṇa 2), the strong persons are अद्मि॑ ádmi, अत्सि॑ átsi, अत्ति॑ átti, and the weak persons are अ॒द्मः admáḥ, अ॒त्थ atthá, अ॒दन्ति॑ adánti, etc.
  • For √सु su “squeeze, press out” (gaṇa 5), the strong persons are सु॒नोमि॑ sunómi, सु॒नोषि॑ sunóṣi, सु॒नोति॑ sunóti, and the weak persons are सु॒नु॒मः sunumáḥ, सु॒न्वन्ति॑ sunvánti, सु॒नु॒ते sunuté (3sg. mid.), सु॒न्वे sunvé (1sg. mid.), etc.

Therefore, what we call the alternation of ablaut (between the stem shapes सुनो- suno- and सुनु- sunu-) is, in reality, an accent-ablaut alternation, in which a strong stem retains its accent while a weak stem throws it onto the personal ending.

In case of √सु su, we see the last syllable of the strong stem is accented, so सु॒नोमि॑ sunómi and others. This is the general rule, but gaṇa 3 behaves differently: here the first syllable of the strong stem (i.e. the reduplicative syllable) receives the accent.
e.g. ददा॑ति dádā-ti, ददा॑सि dádā-si, ददा॑मि dádā-mi, but 3pl. द॒दति॑ dad-áti, 3sg. mid. द॒त्ते dat-té, 1sg. mid. द॒दे dad-é.
Similarly पिप॑र्ति pípar-ti, but 2du. पि॒पृ॒थः pipṛ-tháḥ, 3pl. पि॒प्रति॑ pipr-áti.

The present-tense conjugation of verbs √इ i “go”, √ब्रू brū “speak”, √भृ bhṛ “carry”, √युज् yuj “join” (×2), and √ग्रभ् grabh “grasp” according to the gaṇa-s 2, 2, 3, 7, 7, 9, in one diathesis, follow. The strong forms are put in a box, while unattested forms (in Vedic) are bracketed.
![][image4]
![][image5]
![][image6]
![][image7]
![][image8]
![][image9]

The mobile accent of oxytonic present participles, discussed for thematic verbs above, extends to all athematic verbs. So सु॒न्वन्त्- sunvánt- “squeezing” : सु॒न्वन्त॑म् sunvántam (Acc. sg.) but सु॒न्व॒ता sunvatā́ (Ins. sg.). Below is the chart for अ॒दन्त्- adánt- “eating”.

![][image10]

Exercise : Write the full declension table of भु॒ञ्जन्त्- bhuñjánt- “enjoying”, the present active participle of √भुज् bhuj.

The same mobile accent is also present in the perfect tense for all verbs, whatever be the stem shape at hand. The accent rests on the root in the canonically strong forms (1sg., 2sg., 3sg. in the active voice), elsewhere on the ending. Examples:

  • 1sg. दु॒द्रोह॑ dudróha, 2sg. वि॒व्यक्थ॑ vivyáktha, 3sg. उ॒वाच॑ uvā́ca
  • 1pl. ऊ॒चि॒म ūcimá, 2pl. से॒द sedá, 2du. आ॒वथुः॑ āváthuḥ, 3pl. तु॒ष्टु॒वुः tuṣṭuvúḥ
  • middle 1sg. शे॒पे śepé, 2sg. ज॒ज्ञि॒षे jajñiṣé, 3sg. द॒ध्रे dadhré, 1pl. बु॒भु॒ज्महे॑ bubhujmáhe, 3pl. चि॒कि॒त्रे cikitré

C.3. The accent of nouns and adjectives

is basically determined by the suffix used to make them.

This information is straightforwardly encoded in the Pāṇinian name of the suffix. We give a few examples:

  • The suffix अनीयर् anīyaR has the anubandha र् R, so the rule ६.१.२१७ (उपोत्तमं रिति) {Anuvṛtti उदात्तः} applies. This means the penult (i.e. the second-last syllable) is accented, hence the gerundives क॒र॒णीय॑- karaṇī́ya- “to be done”, आ॒म॒न्त्र॒णीय॑- āmantraṇī́ya- “to be addressed”, etc.

  • By the rule ६.१.१६३ (चितः) {Anuvṛtti अन्त उदात्तः}, the suffixes अठच् aṭhaC, इतच् itaC, कुरच् KuraC, and इमनिच् imanIC produce oxytonic words. For example, क॒र्म॒ठ- karmaṭhá- “diligent”, पु॒ष्पि॒त- puṣpitá- “flowery”, वि॒दु॒र- vidurá- “wise”, म॒हि॒मा mahimā́ “greatness”.

  • The default accent of all words with a suffix, unless otherwise specified, is on the first vowel of the suffix. So e.g. क्त Kta (the verbal adjective) produces oxytonic words : कृ॒त- kṛtá- “done”, शृ॒त- śṛtá- “cooked”, etc. Similarly क्तवतु KtavatU : कृ॒तव॑त्- kṛtávat-, etc.
    This applies, in particular to personal endings of verbs: the correct accentuation is -ánti, -áti, -ánte, -áte, -áta, -áthuḥ.
    An exception is the 3pl. perfect middle ending इरेच् ireC, which is accented -iré (e.g. ई॒जि॒रे ījiré to √यज् yaj) due to the anubandha च् C. It’s a byform of the usual -ré.
    [There’s also the variant -riré, as in ज॒गृ॒भ्रि॒रे jagṛbhriré.]

This portion is covered in the traditional sense in G.V. Devasthali’s 1967 book Anubandhas of Pāṇini [archive.org]. The details are extensive, but it’s not too hard to get a hold of how the system works.

In the modern framework, read either Macdonell’s Vedic Grammar (92ff., 117ff.) [archive.org] or the epochal Altindische Grammatik, Band II,2 [archive.org] .

Here I’m providing a small list of primary (kṛt) suffixes:

  1. The action noun suffix -a- (घञ् GHaÑ) is radically accented, while agentive -a- (अच् aC) is suffixally accented. These employ guṇa of the root.
    e.g. वेद॑- véda- “knowledge” is formed with घञ् GHaÑ while चो॒द- codá- “instigator” is formed with अच् aC.
    Same is the relationship between action and agent -as-. e.g. तर॑स् táras “quickness” vs. त॒रस्- tarás- “quick”; मन॑स् mánas “mind”, वे॒शस्- veśás- “neighbor”.

  2. The gerundive suffix -atá- (Pāṇ. अतच् ataC) is accented as shown: द॒र्श॒त- “visible”, य॒ज॒त- “to be adored”, ह॒र्य॒त- “desirable”.

  3. The action and agent nouns in -ana- accent the root: तप॑न- tápana- “heating”, चोद॑न- códana- “impelling”, देव॑नम् dévanam “play”, रक्ष॑णम् rákṣaṇam “protection”.
    [The synonymous feminine counterpart is -anā́- or -ánā-, e.g. ज॒र॒णा jaraṇā́ “old age”, द्यो॒त॒ना dyotanā́ “brilliance”, व॒धना॑ vadhánā “slaughter”, व॒नना॑ vanánā “desire”.]

  4. Feminine action nouns in -ā́- : ई॒शा īśā́ “dominion”, नि॒न्दा nindā́ “blame”, जि॒गी॒षा jigīṣā́ “desire to win”.
    However, feminine versions of masculine nouns & adjectives (using -ā- or -ī-) simply keep the original accent unchanged. So कृ॒ष्णा- kṛṣṇā́- to कृ॒ष्ण- kṛṣṇá-, मानु॑षी- mā́nuṣī- to मानु॑ष- mā́nuṣa-.
    Important exception: The vṛkī́-suffix [to be discussed later].

  5. The middle participles formed with -āná- generally accent this syllable (except in gaṇa 3 presents), for example

    1. उ॒षा॒ण- uṣāṇá- or व॒सा॒न- vasāná- “wearing” (gaṇa 2)

    2. जुह्वा॑न- júhvāna- “sacrificing” (gaṇa 3)

    3. भि॒न्दा॒न- bhindāná- “splitting” (gaṇa 7)

    4. नि॒दा॒न- nidāná- “reproached” (belonging to the root aorist)

    5. स॒सृ॒जा॒न- sasṛjāná- “being sent forth” (belonging to the perfect)

  6. Since the related -māna- is added to thematic stems, derivatives with that simply retain the accent of the base, e.g. भर॑मान- bháramāna- to भर॑ते bhárate, अ॒ज्यमा॑न- ajyámāna- to अ॒ज्यते॑ ajyáte.

  7. The comparatives in -yāṁs- (and its byform -īyāṁs-) and the superlatives in -iṣṭha- accent the root: नव्यां॑स्- návyāṁs- / नवी॑यांस्- návīyāṁs- “newer”, नवि॑ष्ठ- náviṣṭha- “newest”, श्रेयां॑स्- śréyāṁs- “better”, शवि॑ष्ठ- śáviṣṭha- “most valiant”.

  8. The suffix -nú- : भा॒नुः bhā́nuḥ “brightness”, व॒ग्नुः vagnúḥ “cry, call”, सू॒नुः sūnúḥ “son”, धे॒नुः dhenúḥ “cow”, धृ॒ष्णु- dhṛṣṇú- “bold”.

  9. Derivatives with -rá- / -lá- generally accent the suffix : शु॒क्र- śukra- “bright”, हिं॒स्र- hiṁsrá- “injurious”, रु॒धि॒र- “red”, व॒म्रः vamráḥ “ant”, वी॒रः vīráḥ “man, hero”.
    But exceptions are many, e.g. अज्रः॑ ájraḥ “field”, इन्द्रः॑ índraḥ, वज्रः॑ vájraḥ “thunderbolt”.

![][image11]

C.4. For pronouns and underived avyayá-s

(like इति॑ íti, अ॒भि abhí), not much can be done. Memorize.

Personal as well as demonstrative pronouns regularly accent the final syllable of the stem. For example, though the 1sg. pronoun is अ॒हम् ahám, all other cases are built from a stem in mā̆- : here this very syllable holds the accent, so माम् mā́m, मया॑ máyā, मह्य॑म् máhyam, and so on. The plural — व॒यम् vayám, अ॒स्मान् asmā́n, अ॒स्माभिः॑ asmā́bhiḥ, and so on.

Similarly त्वम् tvám, त्वाम् tvā́m, त्वया॑ tváyā, etc. ;
यु॒वम् yuvám / यु॒वाम् yuvā́m, यु॒वभ्या॑म् yuvábhyām / यु॒वाभ्या॑म् yuvā́bhyām, यु॒वद् yuvád, यु॒वोः yuvóḥ / यु॒वयोः॑ yuváyoḥ.

The declensions of two demonstrative pronouns are given under. The exact same pattern is followed by the relative (य- yá-), interrogative (क- ká-), and reflexive (स्व- svá-) pronouns. Likewise अ॒न्य- anyá-.
![][image12]
![][image13]

The last important aspect of personal pronoun accentuation is that the three cases Accusative, Dative, Genitive have optional enclitic forms, which have no accent. These are the shorter variants like मा॒ mā for माम् mā́m (1sg. Acc.), and मे॒ me for मह्य॑ máhya (1sg. Dat.) as well as for मम॑ máma (1sg. Gen.).

Similarly नो॒ no (1pl.) for these three — अ॒स्मान् asmā́n (Acc.), अ॒स्मभ्यः asmabhyáḥ (Dat.), and अ॒स्माक॑म् asmā́kam (Gen.)

Some examples:

  • यत्त्वेम॑हे॒ प्रति॒ तन्नो॑ जुषस्व॒
    yát tvémahe práti tán no juṣasva [RV]
    “Whatever we ask of you, be pleased to grant it.”

  • मा नो॒ अरा॑तिरीशत
    mā́ no árātir īṣata [RV]
    “Let no malignity prevail against us.”

  • उ॒शना॑ का॒व्यस्त्वा॒ नि होता॑रमसादयत्
    uśanā kāvyaḥ tvā ni hotāram asādayat [RV]
    “Uśanas Kāvya established thee, O Agni, as Invoking Priest.”

  • इ॒ह प्र॒याण॑मस्तु वा॒मिन्द्र॑वायू वि॒मोच॑नम्
    ihá prayā́ṇam astu vām índravāyū vimócanam [RV]
    “Hither, O Indra-Vāyu, be your journey here unyoke your steeds, Here for your draught of Soma juice.”

D. Notation (II) and accent Sandhi {#d.-notation-(ii)-and-accent-sandhi}

D.1. य॒ण्सं॒धिः yaṆ-sandhíḥ

D.1.a. General considerations

When a word or morpheme ending in i or u combines with another starting with a vowel, the i or u becomes a semivowel: y and v, respectively. This is the यण्संधिः (yaṇ-saṁdhi) of Pāṇini. What, then, happens if the i or u were accented?

e.g. Consider the words अ॒भि abhí and अ॒वि॒ता avitā́ standing next to each other. Their juxtaposition without Sandhi is simply अ॒भि अ॑वि॒ता abhí avitā́, where the svaritá induced on the latter a is per the usual rule (UA→US).

Now, by the rules of Sandhi this becomes अभ्यविता abhy avitā in its segmental form. What then happens to the udā́tta vowel of अ॒भि abhí? The answer is that the udā́tta disappears but it leaves its shadow — the svaritá that followed it — behind.

In other words, if we explicitly denote अ॒भि अ॑वि॒ता abhí avitā́ as abhí àvitā́ (with the svaritá à indicated in Romanization with a ‘grave accent mark’), the contracted form shall be abhy àvitā. Now the grave mark cannot be removed from à, i.e. the svaritá has become ‘independent’ (from the preceding udā́tta) so we write abhy àvitā́. In Devanāgarī the process is visually simple: अ॒भि अ॑वि॒ता → अ॒भ्य॑वि॒ता.

This is thus the hallmark of an independent svarita: the sequence AS, or in word-initial position S, since in both cases the udā́tta that could induce a following ánudātta to become svaritá is invisible. A word-initial example is वि ví + अ॒वि॒ता avitā́ → व्य॑वि॒ता vyàvitā́.

How is it to be pronounced, if we’re following the simple rule “udā́tta = high pitch, everything else low pitch”? Add “independent svaritá = falling pitch”, just like the 4th tone of Mandarin, IPA [â] or [a˥˩].
Pronounce the 4th tone in Chinese | Fine-tune your tones

As there are other Sandhi-s that can produce an independent svaritá, this one (coordinate with यण्संधिः) is given the special name of kṣaiprá svaritá (क्षै॒प्रः स्व॑रि॒तः), literally something like “svaritá produced by speaking quickly”.

Some more examples:

  • न॒दी उ॑त्स॒र्गः nadī́ utsargáḥ → न॒द्यु॑त्स॒र्गः nady ùtsargáḥ
  • उ॒रु आ॑दर्दि॒रम् urú ādardirám → उ॒र्वा॑दर्दि॒रम् urv ā̀dardirám

There is one complication left with regard to the kṣaiprá svaritá : Consider the sequence अ॒भि abhí + अ॒ग्निः agníḥ. Here the svaritá is missing on the vowel that would follow the lost accented i ! Because of the following udā́tta in अ॒ग्निः agníḥ, its a remains ánudātta. Yet, the force of यण्संधिः is such that that a does become svaritá: The outcome is abhy àgníḥ.

In Devanāgarī letters, we might be tempted to write अ॒भ्य॑ग्निः, but this way the ग्निः will be read as ánudātta, i.e. in Romanization abhy àgniḥ. So an ad hoc solution is adopted — the kṣaiprá svaritá is also given the ánudātta mark in addition to svaritá (whereby the following vowel, i, becomes udā́tta), and written the following way in Devanāgarī: अ॒भ्य१॒॑ग्निः, where a new grapheme १ bears both the accent marks.

A few more examples:

  • अ॒प्सु अ॒न्तर् apsú antár → अ॒प्स्व१॒॑न्तर् apsv àntár
  • …

In case the vowel which is marked svaritá as well as ánudātta is a long vowel, the grapheme used to carry both is ३, and the ánudātta sign is placed under the vowel as well. So अ॒भि abhí + आ॒श्रेषः॑ āśréṣaḥ → अ॒भ्या॒३॒॑श्रेषः॑ abhy ā̀śréṣaḥ.

D.1.b. यण्संधिः in ū-stem declension

The inflexion of a ū-stem like त॒नू- tanū́- “self” goes as follows, where I’ve split the stem and case-ending for our understanding.

तनूः tanū-ḥ, तन्वम् tanv-am, तन्वा tanv-ā, तन्वे tanv-e, …, तन्वि tanv-i, …, तनूभिः tanū-bhiḥ, …, तनूषु tanū-ṣu

So, in case-endings beginning with a vowel, the stem-final ū remains, while in those beginning with a consonant it becomes v. This is the situation of a kṣaiprá svaritá. Hence the accentuation is

त॒नूः tanū́-ḥ, त॒न्व॑म् tanv-àm, त॒न्वा॑ tanv-ā̀, त॒न्वे॑ tanv-è, …, त॒न्वि॑ tanv-ì, …, त॒नूभिः॑ tanū́-bhiḥ, …, त॒नूषु॑ tanū́-ṣu

The evidence of the Taittirī́ya Sáṁhitā [among others] shows that this is not the only possible outcome. Absence of यण्संधि is also allowed, yielding the forms त॒नुव॑म् tanúvam, त॒नुवा॑ tanúvā, त॒नुवे॑ tanúve, त॒नुवि॑ tanúvi instead of the ones above. But the forms with kṣaiprá svaritá are normative.

Exercise : Write out the full declension tables of वि॒भू- vibhū́- “extensive” and श्व॒श्रू- śvaśrū́- “mother-in-law”.

[Note that all ū-stems are oxytonic (i.e. accented on the final syllable), with just one exception known to me.]

Of course, the ū-stem declension also has variant forms: e.g. Acc. sg. त॒नूम् tanū́m, व॒धूम् vadhū́m are as expected; Dat. sg. त॒न्वै॑ tanvài (VS) = त॒नुवै॑ tanúvai (TB), and so on. There is more to this than meets the eye, but that falls outside the scope of this document.

D.1.c. यण्संधिः in other declensions

The ī-stems fall into two types — those that go like दे॒वी devī́, दे॒वीम् devī́-m, दे॒व्या devy-ā́, दे॒व्यै devy-ái, …, दे॒वीभिः॑ devī́-bhiḥ ;

and those that go like न॒दीः nadī́-ḥ, न॒द्य॑म् nady-àm, न॒द्या॑ nady-ā̀, न॒द्ये॑ nady-è, …, न॒दीभिः॑ nadī́-bhiḥ.

This distinction is not properly laid out in Pāṇinian grammar, and the Pāṇinian ī-stem declension is a singular type that largely continues the Vedic devī́-type.

I won’t stress on the ī-stems more, but just reassert that the nadī́-type puts the kṣaiprá svaritá on the case endings just like the ū-stems do. But the devī́-type is fundamentally different, where यण्संधिः of the oxytonic stem and the ending puts an udā́tta on the ending [as evident above]. The latter is the general rule followed elsewhere in Sanskrit declension where यण्संधिः may arise, e.g. the Ins. sg. of ऊ॒र्मि- ūrmí- “wave” is ऊ॒र्म्या ūrmyā́, not ˣऊ॒र्म्या॑ ˣūrmyā̀.

Even beyond यण्संधिः, the accent-bearing vowel is lost in many cases in declension. Then the ending receives the udā́tta. Some examples are:

  • म॒हि॒मा mahimā́ “greatness” : Ins. sg. म॒हि॒म्ना mahimn-ā́
  • पि॒ता pitā́ “father” : Ins. sg. पि॒त्रा pitr-ā́
  • मू॒र्धा mūrdhā́ “head” : Dat. sg. मू॒र्ध्ने mūrdhn-é
  • अ॒ग्निः agníḥ “fire” : Gen.-Loc. du. अ॒ग्न्योः agny-óḥ

Exercise : Write out the full declension tables of पृ॒थि॒वी- pṛthivī́- “earth” and रात्री॑- rā́trī- “night” following the Classical Sanskrit paradigm [or, optionally, the Vedic devī́-paradigm].

Then do the same for साम॑न्- sā́man- “song” and पि॒तृ- pitṛ́- “father”.

[Optional : Write out the full declension tables of वृ॒की- vṛkī́- “she-wolf” and म॒यू॒री- mayūrī́- “peahen” following the nadī́-paradigm. Note that the nadī́-type ī-stems are all oxytonic.]

D.2. पू॒र्व॒रू॒प॒सं॒धिः pūrvarūpa-sandhíḥ

When accented é or ó contracts with an unaccented a, svarità è or ò emerges. It can be understood in the following way: the new è or ò contains the quality of the first vowel (tongue height & frontness) but the accent of the second — though initially a dependent svaritá, now an independent one.

  • सो अ॑ध॒मः só adhamáḥ → सो॑ऽध॒मः sò ’dhamáḥ
  • पा॒दो अ॑स्य pādó asya → पा॒दो॑ऽस्य pādò ’sya
  • त्वे अ॒धि tvé adhí → त्वे॒३॒॑ऽधि tvè ‘dhi
  • का॒ले अ॑विष्टु kālé aviṣṭu → का॒ले॑ऽविष्टु kālè ’viṣṭu

This is known as the abhiníhita svaritá (अ॒भि॒निहि॑तः स्वरि॒तः), as this Sandhí itself is called abhiníhita.

D.3. Other Sandhi-s

In all other cases, the contraction of two vowels into one is simply udā́tta if either of the precursors was udā́tta, otherwise not.

  • नु॒द॒स्व अथ॑ nudasvá átʰa → नु॒द॒स्वाथ॑ nudasvā́tʰa
  • आ अ॑गात् ā́ agāt → आगा॑त् ā́gāt
  • तव॒ इत् táva ít → तवेत् távét
  • सू॒नवे॒ अग्ने॑ sūnáve ágne → सू॒नवेऽग्ने॑ sūnávé ‘gne
  • पि॒ता इ॑व pitā́ iva → पि॒तेव॑ pitéva
  • श॒ता उ॑ śatā́ u → श॒तो śató
  • न अन्त॑रः ná ántaras → नान्त॑रः nā́ntaras
  • वो॒ अव॑सः vo ávasaḥ → वोऽव॑सः vó ‘vasaḥ

There is a caveat to this: contraction of like vowels (सवर्णदीर्घसंधिः) where only the first one is accented, e.g. í + i, is Pāṇinically allowed to yield both udā́tta (ī́) and svaritá (ī̀). Generally udā́tta is found in case of ā and ū, while svaritá is found for ī, but this differs by śā́khā.

Thus दि॒वि इ॑व diví iva → दि॒वी॑व divī̀va.

In some sense this is an avatar of the kṣaiprá svaritá; though the first i remains a vowel, its udā́tta is lost at the cost of the dependent svaritá on the second i.

This is called the práśliṣṭa svaritá (प्रश्लि॑ष्टः स्वरि॒तः).

D.4. Jātyà svaritá

This is the name given to svaritá that a word has as its primary accent. The word then had no udā́tta, and such words can be counted on the fingers of one hand.

To my knowledge the three words with jātyà svaritá are क्व॑ kvà “where”, क॒न्या॑ kanyā̀ “girl”, and स्व॑र् svàr “sun; heaven; light”,
[स्व॑र् svàr notably appears in Bahuvrīhí compounds like स्व॑र्चक्षस्- svàr-cakṣas- “brilliant as light”.]

Evidence including that from other texts (particularly the Taittirī́ya Sáṁhitā and connected grantha-s) shows that this svaritá likely arose as a byproduct of यण्संधिः. Because in TS the form सुव॑र् súvar appears for स्व॑र् svàr “sun”.

Thus, to summarize, we have two kinds of svaritá : dependent and independent, both of which are marked (अ॑) in Devanāgari. But only the latter is marked (à) in Romanization, and it’s of 4 types: kṣaiprá, práśliṣṭa, abhiníhita, and jātyà (a.k.a. nítya). Some would not count jātyà as a distinct type, but absorb it within kṣaiprá.

E. Compounds

Compounds are an entire can of worms by themselves. We will tackle the most dominant rules that will cover the vast majority of scenarios. All examples given are attested words.

E.1. Tatpuruṣá compounds,

whatever be their formation, generally accent the final syllable.

  • प्र॒थ॒मजा- prathama-jā́- “first-born”
  • स्व॒यं॒भू- svayaṁ-bhū́- “self-existent”
  • अ॒ति॒या॒ज- ati-yājá- “sacrificing excessively”
  • पु॒ष्टि॒म्भ॒र- puṣṭim-bhará- “bringing prosperity”
  • ब॒ल॒वि॒ज्ञा॒य- bala-vijñāyá- “recognized by his strength”
  • अ॒ज्ञा॒त॒य॒क्ष्मः ajñāta-yakṣmáḥ “unknown disease” from यक्ष्मः॑ yákṣmaḥ
  • कृ॒ष्ण॒श॒कु॒नः kṛṣṇa-śakunáḥ “crow” from श॒कु॒नः śakunáḥ
  • जी॒व॒लो॒कः jīva-lokáḥ from लो॒कः lokáḥ
  • इ॒न्द्र॒से॒ना indra-sénā from सेना॑ sénā
  • प॒रि॒पद् pari-pád “snare”

The most important exceptions to this are:

E.1.a. If the second member is a past participle (-ta- or -na-) or an action noun in -ti-, the first member retains its accent.

  • अ॒ग्निनु॑न्न- ｢agní-nunna-｣ “driven away by fire” from अ॒ग्निः agníḥ
  • हस्त॑यत- ｢hásta-yata-｣ from हस्तः॑ hástaḥ
  • स॒धस्तु॑तिः ｢sadhá-stutiḥ｣ “joint praise” from स॒ध sadhá = स॒ह sahá
  • सोम॑सुतिः ｢sóma-sutiḥ｣ from सोमः॑ sómaḥ
  • उदि॑त- ｢úd-ita-｣, दुर्हि॑त- ｢dúr-hita-｣, सहू॑तिः ｢sá-hūtiḥ｣, निर्ऋ॑तिः ｢nír-ṛtiḥ｣

Recall that the accent of simplex past participles and action nouns in -ti- is final, so ग॒त- gatá-, ग॒तिः gatíḥ, कृ॒त- kṛtá-, कृ॒तिः kṛtíḥ, and so on.

E.1.b. The privative prefix a(n)- is always accented.

  • अन॑दन्त्- án-adant- “not eating”
  • अम॑न्यमान- á-manyamāna- “not thinking”
  • अकृ॑त- á-kṛta-, अनू॑ति- án-ūti-, अत॑न्द्र- á-tandra-, अदु॑ष्कृत्- á-duṣkṛt-

E.1.c. Some types of final members pull the accent to the root rather than to the final syllable. These are
(1) Agent nouns in -van- / -man- and -i-

  • र॒घु॒पत्व॑न्- raghu-pátvan- “swift-flying”
  • पु॒रो॒याव॑न्- puro-yā́van- “going in front”
  • सो॒म॒पाव॑न्- soma-pā́van- “Sóma-drinking”
  • सु॒वह्म॑न्- su-váhman- “driving well”
  • प॒थि॒रक्षि॑- pathi-rákṣi- “protecting the road”
  • दु॒र्गृभि॑- dúr-gṛ́bhi- “hard to hold”

Excerpt : VS 27.9
अ॒मु॒त्र॒भूया॒दध॒ यद्य॒मस्य॒ बृह॑स्पते अ॒भिश॑स्ते॒रमु॑ञ्चः …
amutrabhū́yād ádha yád yamásya bṛ́haspate abhíśaster ámuñcaḥ …
“When you, Bṛ́haspatí, freed us from Yamá’s damnation, …”

(2) Action nouns formed with -ya- (neuter)

  • ह॒वि॒रद्य॑म् havir-ádyam “enjoyment of the oblation”
  • पू॒र्व॒पाय्य॑म् pūrva-pā́yyam / पू॒र्व॒पेय॑म् pūrva-péyam “precedence in drinking”
  • स॒ह॒शेय्य॑म् saha-śéyyam “lying together”
  • अ॒मु॒त्र॒भूय॑म् amutra-bhū́yam “being in the other world”
  • रा॒ज॒सूय॑म् rāja-sū́yam “royal consecration”

Curiously, the suffix -yā- (feminine) in the same role produces oxytones.

  • सु॒कृ॒त्या su-kṛtyā́ “righteousness”
  • जा॒त॒वि॒द्या jāta-vidyā́ “innate knowledge”
  • मु॒ष्टि॒ह॒त्या muṣṭi-hatyā́ “fist-fight”, compare अ॒हि॒हत्य॑म् ahi-hátyam “slaying of the serpent”

Excerpt : RV VII 92.1
उपो॑ ते॒ अन्धो॒ मद्य॑मयामि॒ यस्य॑ देव दधि॒षे पू॑र्व॒पेय॑म्
úpo te ándho mádyam ayāmi
yásya deva dadhiṣé pūrvapéyam
“To thee the rapture-bringing juice is offered, whose first draught, God, thou takest as thy portion.”

(3) Action nouns or agentives formed with -ana-

  • दे॒व॒हेळ॑नम् deva-héḷanam “offence against the gods”
  • ब॒द्ध॒क॒मोच॑नम् baddhaka-mócanam “setting free a prisoner”
  • दे॒व॒माद॑न- deva-mā́dana- “exhilarating the gods”
  • के॒श॒वर्ध॑न- keśa-várdhana- “causing the hair to grow”
  • अ॒मि॒त्र॒दम्भ॑न- amitra-dámbhana- “injuring enemies”

E.1.d. If the second member is प॒ति- patí- (which is oxytonic by itself), the first member is generally accented. This extends to पत्नी॑- pátnī- as well.

So गृ॒हप॑तिः gṛhá-patiḥ, गोप॑तिः gó-patiḥ, वाज॑पतिः vā́ja-patiḥ from गृ॒हः gṛháḥ, गौः gáuḥ, वाजः॑ vā́jaḥ, and so on.

There are exceptions, the most important being वि॒श्पतिः॑ viś-pátiḥ “chief of the tribe” (and वि॒श्पत्नी॑ viś-pátnī). There are a number of exceptions in some other Sáṁhitā-s, so the Atharvavedá has प॒शु॒पतिः॑ paśu-pátiḥ and अ॒प्स॒रा॒प॒तिः apsarā-patíḥ.

E.2. Bahuvrīhí compounds

The first member is accented on its original syllable.

  • अश्व॑पृष्ठ- áśva-pṛṣṭha- “borne on horseback” from अश्वः॑ áśvaḥ
  • प॒र्जन्य॑रेतस्- parjánya-retas- “sprung from the seed of Parjánya” from प॒र्जन्यः॑ parjányaḥ
  • वी॒रप॑स्त्य- vīrá-pastya- “belonging to the abode of a hero” from वी॒रः vīráḥ
  • श॒तशा॑रद- śatá-śārada- “lasting a hundred autumns” from श॒तम् śatám
  • इ॒द्धाग्नि॑- iddhā́-’gniḥ- “whose fire is kindled” from इ॒द्ध- iddhá-
  • इन्द्र॑ज्येष्ठ- índra-jyeṣṭha- “whose chief is Indra” from इन्द्रः॑ índraḥ
  • यु॒यु॒जा॒नस॑प्ति- yuyujāná-sapti- “whose steeds are yoked” from यु॒यु॒जा॒न- yuyujāná-, perfect middle participle of √युज् yuj
  • व॒र्षाज्य॑- varṣā́-’jya- “whose rain is like butter” from व॒र्षा varṣā́ or व॒र्षम् varṣám
  • वृ॒क्षके॑श- vṛkṣá-keśa- “tree-haired” i.e. “wooded” (said of a mountain), from वृ॒क्षः vṛkṣáḥ
  • दि॒वियो॑नि- diví-yoni- “having origin in heaven” from दि॒वि diví, Locative of द्यौः dyáuḥ “sky”

E.2.a. The privative prefix a(n)- always throws the accent onto the final syllable.
[This exactly mirrors the Tatpuruṣá situation.]

  • अ॒दन्त्- a-dánt- “toothless”
  • अ॒फ॒ल- a-phalá- “fruitless” from फल॑म् phálam
  • अ॒ब॒ल- a-balá- “weak” from बल॑म् bálam

The abstract nouns in -ti- constitute an exception to this, e.g. अक्षि॑ति- á-kṣiti- “imperishable” (but also a Tatpuruṣá compound “imperishablebess”).

E.2.b. Bahuvrīhí-s beginning with dus- and su- regularly accent the second member, usually on its original syllable.

  • दु॒र्मन्म॑न्- dur-mánman- “ill-disposed” from मन्म॑ mánma “thought”
  • दु॒र्णाम॑न्- dur-ṇā́man- from नाम॑ nā́ma
  • सु॒भग॑- su-bhága- “well endowed” from भगः॑ bhágaḥ “welfare”
  • सु॒प॒र्ण- su-parṇá- from प॒र्णम् parṇám “feather”
  • सु॒पा॒णि- su-pāṇí- from पा॒णिः pāṇíḥ “hand”
  • सु॒शृङ्ग॑- su-śṛ́ṅga- from शृङ्ग॑म् śṛ́ṅgam “horn”

In case of su- there’s a tendency to throw the accent forward on the final syllable.

  • स्व॒ङ्गु॒रि- sv-aṅgurí- from अ॒ङ्गुरि॑- aṅgúriḥ “finger”
  • सु॒फल॑- su-phála- (RV) but सु॒फ॒ल- su-phalá- (AV), from फल॑म् phálam “fruit”
  • सु॒बन्धु॑- su-bándhu- (RV) but सु॒ब॒न्धु- su-bandhú- (AV), from बन्धुः॑ bándhuḥ “friend”

Excerpt : RV IV 54.4
यत्पृ॑थि॒व्या वरि॑म॒न्ना स्व॑ङ्गु॒रिर्वर्ष्म॑न्दि॒वः सु॒वति॑ स॒त्यम॑स्य॒ तत्
yát pṛthivyā́ várimann ā́ svaṅgurír várṣman diváḥ suváti satyám asya tát
“What the fair-fingered God (=Savitā́) brings forth on earth’s expanse or in the height of heaven, that work of his stands sure.”

However, ku- is seen to follow the ordinary Bahuvrīhí accent, e.g. कुय॑व- kú-yava- “causing a bad harvest”.

E.2.c. When the first member is विश्व॑- víśva- “all” (and optionally सर्व॑- sárva-), its second syllable is accented.

  • वि॒श्वपे॑शस्- viśvá-peśas- “having all adornment”
  • स॒र्वज॑न्मन्- sarvá-janman- “of all kinds”
  • स॒र्वायु॑स्- sarvā́-’yus- “having all life”

E.2.d. When the first member is a disyllabic adjective, the second member is accented — as a rule on its original accented syllable, but sometimes on the final syllable.

  • पु॒रु॒पु॒त्र- puru-putrá- “having many sons”, from पु॒त्रः putráḥ
  • कृ॒धु॒कर्ण॑- kṛdhu-kárṇa- “having short ears”, from कर्णः॑ kárṇaḥ
  • ब॒ह्व॒न्न- bahv-anná- “rich in food”, from अन्न॑म् ánnam
  • शि॒ति॒पद्- śiti-pád- “white-footed”
  • उ॒रु॒क्षय॑- uru-kṣáya- “having a wide abode”, from क्षयः॑ kṣáyaḥ
  • ऋ॒जु॒क्रतु॑- ṛju-krátu- “whose works are right”
  • तु॒वि॒द्यु॒म्न- tuvi-dyumná- “having great glory”
  • पृ॒थु॒पक्ष॑स्- pṛthu-pákṣas- “broad-chested”
  • हि॒रि॒शि॒प्र- hiri-śiprá- “golden-cheeked”

This is optional in the sense that there’s an increasing tendency to follow the general Bahuvrīhí rule in the later Sáṁhitā-s.
e.g. पु॒रुणा॑मन् purú-ṇāman-, शिति॑ककुद्- śíti-kakud-, शिति॑भसद्- śíti-bhasad-.

E.3. Other compounds

E.3.a. In an iterative (āmreḍita) compound, the second word is unaccented.

  • अह॑रहर् áhar ahar, दि॒वे दि॑वे divé dive “every day”
  • गृ॒हे गृ॑हे gṛhé gṛhe, दमे॑ दमे dáme dame “in every house”
  • दि॒शो दि॑शः diśó diśaḥ “from every direction”
  • अन्न॑मन्नम् annam annam “food in perpetuity”
  • अङ्गा॑दङ्गात् áṅgād aṅgāt “from every limb”
  • पर्व॑णि पर्वणि párvaṇi parvaṇi “in every joint”

Excerpt : AV 10.6.5
तस्मै॑ घृ॒तं सुरां॒ मध्वन्न॑मन्नं क्षदामहे।
स नः॑ पि॒तेव॑ पु॒त्रेभ्यः॒ श्रेयः॑ श्रेयश्चिकित्सतु॒ भूयो॑भूयः॒ श्वःश्वो॑ दे॒वेभ्यो॑ म॒णिरेत्य॑ ॥
tásmai ghṛtáṃ súrāṃ mádhv ánnam-annam kṣadāmahe ।
sá naḥ pitéva putrébhyaḥ śréyaḥ-śreyaś cikitsatu bhū́yo-bhūyaḥ śváḥ-śvo devébhyo maṇír étya ॥
“To it (=the amulet, maṇi) we distribute ghee, strong drink, honey, food after food; for us, as a father for his sons, let it provide what is better and better, more and more, morrow after morrow — the amulet, coming from the gods.”

E.3.b. The development of the copulative (dvandva) compound in Sanskrit is long and complex. But when it comes to accent, there are just two possibilities:

  1. Both members are accented in their original position. This is restricted to the archaic Devatā-dvandvas, which must have the dual ending in both members.
  2. The final syllable is accented.

To the first type belong

  • इन्द्रा॒बृह॒स्पती॑ Índrā-Bṛ́haspátī
  • अ॒ग्नीसोमा॑ Agnī́-Sómā
  • मि॒त्रयो॒र्वरु॑णयोः Mitráyor-Váruṇayoḥ (Genitive-Locative)
  • द्यावा॑पृथि॒वी Dyā́vā-Pṛthivī́
    [Recall that पृ॒थि॒वी Pṛthivī́ is using the proper dual ending of devī́-type ī-stems in Vedic, not पृ॒थि॒व्यौ Pṛthivyáu — which would work in the Classical language.]

The two words may be split, as in
Extract : RV V 45.4
सू॒क्तेभि॑र्वो॒ वचो॑भिर्दे॒वजु॑ष्टै॒रिन्द्रा॒ न्व१॒॑ग्नी अव॑से हु॒वध्यै॑
sūktébhir vo vácobhir devájuṣṭair
índrā nv àgnī́ ávase huvádhyai
“With hymns and God-loved words will I invoke you, Indra and Agni, to obtain your favour.”

Extract : RV VII 42.5
आ नक्ता॑ ब॒र्हिः स॑दतामु॒षासो॒शन्ता॑ मि॒त्रावरु॑णा यजे॒ह
ā́ náktā barhíḥ sadatām uṣā́sā ‿
uśántā mitrā́váruṇā yajehá
“Here on our sacred grass let Night and Dawn be seated: bring longing Varuṇa and Mitra hither.”

The second rule (“final syllable accented”) can be applied to all Dvandva-s.

  • इ॒न्द्र॒वा॒यू Indra-Vāyū́
  • स॒त्या॒नृ॒ते satyā-’nṛté “truth & untruth”
  • ऋ॒क्सा॒माभ्या॑म् ṛk-sāmā́bhyām “with hymns & chant”
  • शू॒द्रा॒र्यौ śūdrā-’ryáu
  • पि॒ता॒पु॒त्रौ pitā-putráu “father & son”

F. The sentence accent

We have seen how all words that fundamentally have an udā́tta continue to possess that in a sentence, while the ánudātta-s and svaritá-s are subject to influence from adjacent syllables even across words.
For example, नम॑स् námas followed by हस्तः॑ hástaḥ yields नमो॒ हस्तः॑ námo hástaḥ, where the svaritá of नम॑स् becomes an ánudātta due to the following udā́tta (of हस्तः॑ hástaḥ).

F.1. Finite verbs

A peculiar phenomenon is that finite verbs are regularly left unaccented in a sentence, unless starting the sentence. So “Rāma eats” is रा॒मः खा॑दति rāmáḥ khādati, though खादति khādati does have a grammatically determined accent, viz. खाद॑ति khā́dati.
But खाद॑ति रा॒मः khā́dati rāmáḥ.

Finite verbs do get accented in relative clauses, the most important by far being “if…” clauses and clauses introduced by य- yá- “who, which”.
So “If Sītā cooks, Rāma eats” will be written
रा॒मः खा॑दति॒ यदि॒ सीता॒ पच॑ति rāmáḥ khādati yádi sī́tā pácati
or सीता॒ पच॑ति॒ चेद्रा॒मः खा॑दति sī́tā pácati céd rāmáḥ khādati.

At the same time, any preverb attached to the verb (like प्र prá, नि ní, अधि ádhi) — which is normally accented — loses its accent in the subordinate clause.
e.g. सीता॑ सं॒पच॑ति॒ चेद्रा॒मः प्रखा॑दति sī́tā saṁ-pácati céd rāmáḥ prá-khādati.
[But a preverb detached from the verb does not suffer any loss of accent.
e.g. सीता॒ सं सू॒पं पच॑ति॒ चेद्रा॒मः प्रखा॑दति sī́tā sáṁ sūpáṁ pácati céd rāmáḥ prá-khādati.]

Examples from the literature with relative conjunctions — यद् yád and यदि॑ yádi “if”, यथा॑ yáthā “how”, य॒दा yadā́ “when”, etc.

  • यर्हि॑ प्र॒जाः क्षुधं॑ नि॒गच्छे॑यु॒स्तर्हि॑ नवरा॒त्रेण॑ य॒जे॒त॒ (TS)
    yárhi prajā́ḥ kṣúdhaṁ ni-gáccheyus tárhi navarātréṇa yajeta
    “When his people be exposed to hunger, then he should sacrifice with the rite of nine nights.”
    We see यर्हि॑ … नि॒गच्छे॑युस् … तर्हि॑ … यजेत,
    i.e. yárhi … ni-gáccheyus … tárhi yajeta.

  • स य॒दा सं॑ग्रा॒मं जये॒दथै॑न्द्रा॒ग्नं निर्व॑पेत् (MS)
    sá yadā́ saṁgrāmáṁ jáyed átha‿ aindrāgnáṁ nír-vapet
    “As soon as he may have won a battle, he should sacrifice to Indrāgnī.”
    We see य॒दा … जये॑द् … अथ॑‿ … निर्व॑पेत्,
    i.e. yadā́ … jáyed … átha‿ … nír-vapet.

  • उषो॒ यद॒द्य भा॒नुना॒ वि द्वारा॑वृ॒णवो॑ दि॒वः, प्र नो॑ यच्छतादवृ॒कम् (RV I 48.15)
    úṣo yád adyá bhānúnā ví dvā́rāv r̥ṇávo diváḥ, prá no yacchatād avr̥kám
    “O Dawn, when today with thy beam thou shalt open the doors of heaven, then bestow on us safe shelter.”
    We see यद् … वि … ऋ॒णवो॑ … प्र … य॒च्छ॒ता॒द्,
    i.e. yád … ví … ṛṇávo … prá … yacchatād.

  • ए॒तं चेद॒न्यस्मा॑ अनुब्रू॒यास्तत॑ ए॒व ते॒ शिर॑श्छिन्द्याम् (ŚB)
    etáṁ céd anyásmā anu-brūyā́s táta evá te śíraś chindyām
    “If you were to communicate this to another, I’d cut off your head.”
    We see चेद् … अ॒नु॒ब्रू॒यास्तत॑‿ … छि॒न्द्या॒म्
    i.e. céd … anu-brūyā́s táta‿ … chindyām

  • य॒दा क॒दा च॑ सु॒नवा॑म॒ सोम॑म्, अ॒ग्निष्ट्वा॑ दू॒तो ध॑न्वा॒त्यच्छ॑ (RV III 53.4)
    “Whenever we shall have pressed Soma, Agni shall hasten to thee as a messenger.”
    We see य॒दा क॒दा च॑ सु॒नवा॑म … ध॒न्वा॒ति॒‿
    i.e. yadā́ kadā́ ca sunávāma … dhanvāti‿.

  • वि चेदु॒च्छन्त्य॑श्विना उ॒षासः॒ प्र वां॒ ब्रह्मा॑णि का॒रवो॑ भरन्ते (VII 72.4)
    “When the Dawns shine forth, O Aśvins, the singers offer their prayers to you.”
    We see वि चेदु॒च्छन्ति॑‿… प्र … भ॒र॒न्ते॒,
    i.e. ví céd ucchánti … prá … bharante.

Examples with relative clauses — introduced by यः yáḥ and its forms.

  • यस्तुभ्यं॒ दाशा॒न्न तमंहो॑ अश्नवत् (RV II 23.4)
    “Who shall serve thee, him no distress can touch.”
    We see यस् … दाशा॑न् … अ॒श्न॒व॒त्,
    i.e. yás … dā́śān … aśnavat.

  • उ॒त नू॒नं यदि॑न्द्रि॒यं क॑रि॒ष्या इ॑न्द्र॒ पौंस्य॑म्, अ॒द्या नकि॒ष्टदा मि॑नत् (RV IV 30.23)
    “And what heroic, manly deed thou, Indra, shalt now do, that let no one belittle today.”
    We see यद् … क॒रि॒ष्या … मि॒न॒त्
    i.e. yád … kariṣyā́ … minat.

  • ओ ते य॑न्ति॒ ये अ॑प॒रीषु॒ पश्या॑न् (RV I 113.11)
    ó té yanti yé aparī́ṣu páśyān
    “Those are coming who in future days will see her.”
    We see आ‿ … ते य॑न्ति॒ ये … पश्या॑न्
    i.e. ā́ … té yanti yé … páśyān.

  • यस्मै॒ त्वं सु॒कृते॑ जातवेद, उ लो॒कम॑ग्ने कृ॒णवः॑ स्यो॒नम् … स … र॒यिं न॑शते स्व॒स्ति (RV V 4.11)
    yásmai tváṁ sukŕ̥te jātaveda, u lokám agne kr̥ṇávaḥ syonám … sá … rayíṁ naśate svastí
    “The righteous man for whom thou shalt procure, O Agni Jātavedas, a comfortable place, he obtains riches for welfare.”
    We see यस्मै॑ … कृ॒णवः॑ … स… न॒श॒ते॒,
    i.e. yásmai … kṛṇávaḥ … sá … naśate.

  • अग्ने॒ यं य॒ज्ञम॑ध्व॒रं वि॒श्वतः॑ परि॒भूरसि॑ । स इद्दे॒वेषु॑ गच्छति ॥ (RV I 1.4)
    ágne yáṁ yajñám adhvaráṁ viśvátaḥ paribhū́r ási । sá íd devéṣu gacchati ॥
    The perfect sacrifice, which thou, Agni, encompassest about, verily goeth to the Gods.
    We see यं … प॒रि॒भूरसि॑ … स … ग॒च्छ॒ति॒,
    i.e. yáṁ … paribhū́r ási … sá … gacchati.

Aṣṭādhyāyī : Major sū́tra-s concerning our topic are these. But the entire Pāda (८.१) is relevant.

  • ८.१.२८ तिङ्ङतिङः
    Adhikāra अनुदात्तं सर्वमपादादौ
  • ८.१.३० निपातैर्यद्यदिहन्तकुविन्नेच्चेच्चण्कच्चिद्यत्रयुक्तम्
  • ८.१.३५ छन्दस्यनेकमपि साकाङ्क्षम्
  • ८.१.३६ यावद्यथाभ्याम्
  • ८.१.५६ यद्धितुपरं छन्दसि
  • ८.१.६६ यद्वृत्तान्नित्यम्
  • ८.१.७१ तिङि चोदात्तवति

Exercise : Translate the following into accented Sanskrit.

  1. If he speaks, I will listen.
  2. The wise student who diligently studies the scriptures every day in the forest, will certainly attain knowledge.
  3. Wherever the river flows, trees grow.
  4. Because the cruel king oppressed the poor people of the village, the brave warriors destroyed his army.
  5. Whatever wealth you give to the poor, that is your true merit.
  6. When the grey horse runs swiftly in the field, then the children watch with joy.
  7. As the mother cooks the food, so the daughter eats it.
  8. Although the heavy rain falls continuously today, yet the diligent farmer goes to his field.
  9. Whoever brings the lost book here, he will receive a reward.
  10. The deer wander freely in the peaceful forest whenever birds sing sweetly in the trees.

F.2. Vocatives

Vocatives are unaccented — again, except when starting a sentence (or pāda). But, even more exceptionally, they’re underlyingly unaccented; hence, it’s the first syllable that receives the accent when starting a sentence, irrespective of the word’s original accent.

  • अग्ने॑ सूपाय॒नो भ॑व (RV I.1.9)
    ágne sūpāyanó bhava
    even though the word is originally oxytonic: अ॒ग्निः agníḥ.
  • होत॑र्यविष्ठ सुक्रतो (RV IV.4.11)
    hótar yaviṣṭha sukrato
    [an entire chain of Vocatives starting a sentence or pāda]

Aṣṭādhyāyī :

  • ८.१.१९ आमन्त्रितस्य च
    Adhikāra अनुदात्तं सर्वमपादादौ
  • ८.१.७२ आमन्त्रितं पूर्वमविद्यमानवत्

G. Other verb forms

We have covered the present (लट्), future (लृट्), and perfect (लिट्).

G.1. The augmented tenses

— i.e. those formed with the augment a- — accent that. They all indicate past time.

  1. the imperfect (लङ्), e.g. अभ॑रत् ábharat “he carried”, असु॑नोत् ásunot “he extracted”, असु॑न्वन् ásunvan “they extracted”
  2. the aorist (लुङ्), whatever be its formation, e.g. अक॑र् ákar “he did”, अरु॑धत् árudhat “he obstructed”, अजी॑जनत् ájījanat “he begot”, अक्र॑मिष्ट ákramiṣṭa “he walked”
  3. the pluperfect, …

However, all of these also come unaugmented, then called the injunctive mood which is its own subject of study. For our purposes its greatest use is with the prohibitive particle मा mā́, e.g. मा कः॑ mā́ kaḥ “don’t do (it)”.

  1. The imperfect (लङ्) accents the same syllable as the corresponding present (लट्). So for thematic verbs 3sg. भर॑त् like भर॑ति, 3pl. भर॑न् like भर॑न्ति, 3sg. तु॒दत् like तु॒दति॑, etc.
    For athematic verbs सु॒नोत् sunót like सु॒नोति॑ sunóti, सु॒न्वन् sunván like सु॒न्वन्ति॑ sunvánti, सु॒नु॒त sunutá like सु॒नु॒ते sunuté.

  2. The root aorist follows the same principle as the root present (gaṇa 1): accented radix in the strong forms, दात् dā́t “he put”, दाः dā́ḥ, दाम् dā́m ; and accented ending in the weak forms, दा॒म dāmá, दा॒त dātá, दुः dúḥ.

  3. The a-aorist (=thematic root aorist) and sa-aorist, like the gaṇa 4 present, accents the thematic vowel.
    e.g. भु॒जत् bhuját, भु॒जाम॑ bhujā́ma, etc.

  4. The s- and iṣ-aorists accent the root.

  5. The reduplicated aorist accents the root, but sometimes the reduplicative syllable. Pāṇini allows both (६.१.२१८ चङ्यन्यतरस्याम्).

  6. The pluperfect accent the reduplicative syllable (like the gaṇa 3 present) if it’s athematic, but the thematicized vowel if it’s thematic.

G.2. The subjunctive (लेट्) and imperative (लोट्) {#g.2.-the-subjunctive-(लेट्)-and-imperative-(लोट्)}

simply accent the vowel on which the strong stem of the indicative is accented. In practice this simply means the vowel right before the subjunctive modal sign, in case of athematic stems.
In thematic stems it has no say: the accent of the base (as the case may be) is continued.

Thus, in the present system, we get
thematic भरा॑त् bhárāt (to भर॑ति bhárati), तु॒दात् tudā́t (to तु॒दति॑ tudáti);
athematic सु॒नव॑त् sunávat (to सु॒नोति॑ sunóti), सु॒नव॑न् sunávan (to सु॒न्वन्ति॑ sunvánti).

Likewise the imperative (लोट्) formation:
thematic भर॑तु bháratu “carry!”, तु॒दतु॑ tudátu “push!”;
athematic सु॒नोतु॑ sunótu “extract!”, सु॒न्वन्तु॑ sunvántu

If you are only interested in the Classical language, ignore the following.

In the aorist system, we get the subjunctives
athematic श्रव॑त् śrávat (to श्रोत् śrót), श्रव॑न् śrávan (to श्रु॒वन् śruván);
यंस॑त् yáṁsat (to यान् yā́n), यंस॑न् yáṁsan (to यंसुः॑ yáṁsuḥ);
निन्दि॑षत् níndiṣat (to निन्दी॑त् níndīt), निन्दि॑षन् níndiṣan (to निन्दि॑षन् níndiṣuḥ);
thematic भु॒जात् bhujā́t to भु॒जत् bhuját, धु॒क्षात् dhukṣā́t to धु॒क्षत् dhukṣát.

In the perfect system, we get
ज॒घन॑त् jaghánat (to ज॒घान॑ jaghā́na), ज॒घन॑न् jaghánan (to ज॒घ्नुः jaghnúḥ);
व॒वर्ते॑ vavárte (to व॒वृ॒ते vavṛté), व॒वर्त॑न्त vavártanta (to व॒वृ॒त्रे vavṛtre).

G.3. The optative (लिङ्) {#g.3.-the-optative-(लिङ्)}

is an ablauting formation, whose accent shifts with ablaut when attached to athematic stems. The strong optative modal sign (-yā́-) is accented, while the weak one (-ī-) throws the accent onto the ending. For thematic verbs the accent of the verbal base is, naturally, retained.
[Recall that the strong optative modal sign (-yā́-) is used in Sanskrit for the entire active voice except the 3pl.]

Thus, in the present system, we get
thematic भरे॑त् bháret (to भर॑ति bhárati), तु॒देत् tudét (to तु॒दति॑ tudáti);
athematic सु॒नु॒यात् sunuyā́t (to सु॒नोति॑ sunóti), सु॒नु॒युः sunuyúḥ (to सु॒न्वन्ति॑ sunvánti).

In the aorist system, we get
athematic धे॒याम् dheyā́m (to धाम् dhā́m), धे॒याम॑ dheyā́ma (to धा॒म dhāmá);
अ॒शी॒त aśītá (to अ॒ष्ट aṣṭá), अ॒शी॒रन् aśīrán (to अ॒शत॑ aśáta);
thematic भु॒जेत् bhujét to भु॒जत् bhuját, धु॒क्षेत् dhukṣét to धु॒क्षत् dhukṣát.

In the perfect system, we get
रि॒रि॒च्याम् riricyā́m (to रि॒रेच॑ riréca), रि॒रि॒च्याम॑ riricyā́ma (to रि॒रि॒चि॒म riricimá);
व॒वृ॒ती॒य vavṛtīyá (to व॒वृ॒ते vavṛté), व॒वृ॒ती॒महि॑ vavṛtīmáhi (to व॒वृ॒त्महे॑ vavṛtmáhe).

Excerpt : RV I 116.2
उ॒त पश्य॑न्नश्नु॒वन्दी॒र्घमायु॒रस्त॑मि॒वेज्ज॑रि॒माणं॑ जगम्याम्
utá páśyann aśnuván dīrghám ā́yur ।
ástam ivéj jarimā́ṇaṁ jagamyām ॥
“May I, enjoying lengthened life, still seeing, enter old age as ’twere the house I live in.”

G.4. Non-finite verb forms

We have already treated the active and middle participles.

G.3.a. As for the infinitive in -tum (the only one in Classical Sanskrit), the accent is radical, so कर्तु॑म् kártum “to do”, प्रष्टु॑म् “to ask”, etc. If a preverb is present, that gets accented, e.g. उन्ने॑तुम् ún-netum “to rescue”.
The same accentuation is followed by the Vedic infinitives in -tave, -tos.

The most common infinitive formation in the Ṛgveda is -e, which accents the suffix if simplex (e.g. दृ॒शे dṛśé “to see”) but the root if compounded (e.g. आ॒तुजे॑ ā-túje “to strike out”). In the infinitives in -dhyai, the connecting vowel (-a-) is generally accented, e.g. स्त॒वध्यै॑ stavádhyai “to praise”, मा॒द॒यध्यै॑ mādayádhyai “to exhilarate”.

G.3.b. The gerundives in -ya and -tva accent the root, while those in -ā́yya, -énya, -anī́ya, and -tavyà accent the suffix as shown. Preverbs have no effect.

  1. श्रुत्य॑- śrútya- “to be heard”, ईड्य॑- ī́ḍya- “to be lauded”, प्र॒ति॒चक्ष्य॑- prati-cákṣya- “visible”
  2. जेत्व॑- jétva- “to be gained”, कर्त्व॑- kártva- “to be done”
  3. श्र॒वाय्य॑- śravā́yya- “glorious”, वि॒दाय्य॑- vidā́yya- “to be found”
  4. ई॒क्षे॒ण्य॑- īkṣéṇya- “to be seen”, स॒प॒र्येण्य॑- saparyéṇya- “to be worshipped”
  5. उ॒प॒जी॒व॒नीय॑- upa-jīvanī́ya- “affording livelihood”, आ॒ह॒व॒नीय॑- ā-havanī́ya “to be offered as an oblation”
  6. ज॒नि॒त॒व्य॑- janitavyà- “to be born”, क॒र्त॒व्य॑- kartavyà- “to be done”

G.3.c. The gerunds (or “absolutives”) in -ya accent the root; the others accent the suffix.

  1. अ॒भि॒गूर्य॑ abhi-gū́rya “having welcomed”, सं॒गृभ्य॑ saṁ-gṛ́bhya “having grabbed”, आ॒गत्या॑ ā-gátyā “having come”
  2. यु॒क्त्वा yuktvā́, ह॒त्वा hatvā́, क॒ल्प॒यि॒त्वा kalpayitvā́, प्र॒त्य॒र्प॒यि॒त्वा praty-arpayitvā́
  3. दृ॒ष्ट्वाय॑ dṛṣṭvā́ya , ब॒द्ध्वाय॑ baddhvā́ya
  4. कृ॒त्वी kṛtvī́, गू॒ढ्वी gūḍhvī́, स्क॒भि॒त्वी skabhitvī́

The “simultaneous absolutives” in -am accent the root.
[For this formation, see Aṣṭādhyāyī ३.४.२२ आभीक्ष्ण्ये णमुल् च, and a number of other sū́tra-s in this Pāda.]
e.g. प॒रि॒क्राम॑म् pari-krā́mam “while treading around”, वि॒ग्राह॑म् vi-grā́ham “while separating successively”, अन॑पेक्षम् án-ape-’kṣam “without looking aside”.

Appendix: References

Hyperlinks are provided for convenience. Some may get outdated; other issues may arise.

  1. Comprehensive works on grammar and/or accent [in a very rough order of importance + recommended reading]:

    1. Macdonell’s Vedic Grammar [1910]

    2. Macdonell’s A Vedic Grammar for Students [1916]
      For syntax – which is not covered in his Vedic Grammar.

    3. Altindische Grammatik
      Most comprehensive for the topics it does cover. Most importantly, its volumes on Nominal Composition (II,1) and Nominal Suffixes (II,2).

    4. Goto’s Old Indo-Aryan Morphology and its Indo-Iranian Background [2013]
      A short but modern work (unlike the previous three), hence it incorporates a lot of the development in our understanding of Vedic grammar.

    5. Haug’s Ueber das Wesen und den Werth des wedischen Accents [1872]
      To understand the various systems of marking the Vedic accent.

    6. Lindner’s Antindische Nominalbildung [1878]
      For some, a useful catalogue of formations, arranged by suffix.

    7. Arnold’s Vedic Metre in its Historical Development [1905]

    8. Speyer’s Vedische und Sanskrit-Syntax [1896]

  2. VedaWeb
    To read the Ṛgveda [with several translations available], Śatapatha Brāhmaṇa, and some other Vedic texts.
    Many of the VedaWeb hyperlinks in this document don’t point to the indicated verse as the website’s structure has changed sometime in early 2026. Hence, please find the verse in question manually.

  3. Devasthali’s Anubandhas of Pāṇini [1967]
    A useful guide to extract the most out of the Aṣṭādhyāyī.
    Meanwhile the स्वरप्रकरणम्‌ of सिद्धान्तकौमुदी (Wikisource link) collects the accent-oriented sū́tra-s of the Aṣṭādhyāyī.

इति॑ रूपरे॒खा समा॑प्ता
íti rūparekhā́ sámāptā
